/bin/bash -lc 'timeout 15s nl -ba snapback4/crates/snapback4-lang/src/guide/rule_recipes_wave15.md' in /home/ccheever/projects/sb4-w15-rules5
 succeeded in 0ms:
     1	
     2	## Totals over the caller's readable rows
     3	
     4	`guide rule-recipes readable-totals` pins the owner that decides the whole
     5	source rule. Hidden rows never enter this caller's count or sum. A shared
     6	total is a separate disclosure decision (`guide totals`); do not remove the
     7	owner pin to make a capacity check over private rows.
     8	
     9	```quarry
    10	use personas alice, bob, carol
    11	table personalCosts:
    12	  owner: principal
    13	  cents: int 0..1000000
    14	  immutable owner
    15	  read <- .owner = viewer
    16	  insert <- .owner = viewer
    17	  online only
    18	mutation addPersonalCost(cents: int 0..1000000):
    19	  return insert personalCosts { owner: viewer, cents }
    20	query readableTotals():
    21	  return { n: count(personalCosts where .owner = viewer), cents: sum(personalCosts where .owner = viewer, .cents) }
    22	```
    23	
    24	These online totals cover the complete authorized domain or return unavailable
    25	at a meter cut. They never return a partial number. For an arbitrary mixture
    26	of partly readable rows, page the rows and label a sum of that page as a page
    27	subtotal, or materialize a separately authorized view.
    28	
    29	## Presence-gated durable writes with disconnect grace
    30	
    31	`guide rule-recipes presence-write` requires an enrolled peer and the server's
    32	`connected` verdict before recording a durable handoff. Presence belongs in
    33	the mutation, since table rules cannot read it. Durable rules independently
    34	restrict the writer and recipient. This is a server decision: the mutation
    35	queues offline and predicts no handoff. Ephemeral room indicators cannot
    36	authorize durable writes (`guide ephemeral`).
    37	
    38	```quarry
    39	use personas alice, bob, carol
    40	table handoffPeers:
    41	  principal: principal
    42	  unique principal
    43	  read <- .principal = viewer or viewer = 'service:ops'
    44	  online only
    45	table durableHandoffs:
    46	  sender: principal
    47	  recipient: principal
    48	  body: text 1..200
    49	  immutable sender, recipient, body
    50	  read <- .sender = viewer or .recipient = viewer
    51	  insert <- .sender = viewer and exists handoffPeers[.recipient]
    52	  online only
    53	mutation handoff(recipient: principal, body: text 1..200):
    54	  require exists connected[recipient] else PEER_AWAY
    55	  return insert durableHandoffs { sender: viewer, recipient, body }
    56	query handoffs(c: cursor ?):
    57	  return durableHandoffs where .sender = viewer first 20 by .id after c
    58	query handoffPresence(recipient: principal):
    59	  return exists connected[recipient]
    60	```
    61	
    62	An open authenticated change poll counts as connected. After it ends, a
    63	35-second grace bridges poll turnover and short disconnects. A handoff inside
    64	that grace is intentionally accepted; after it expires it gets `PEER_AWAY`.
    65	Reconnecting starts a new connected interval. Presence is a snapshot at the
    66	server's decision, not an acknowledgement that a person saw the message, a
    67	delivery guarantee, or a lock on their future connection. The row survives
    68	disconnect and server restart. Use a recipient-written durable receipt for
    69	acknowledgement. Test grace with `POST /dev/clock/advance` and
    70	`{ "duration": "36s" }` after the peer's poll has ended.
    71	
    72	## Scheduled publication, withdrawal and reapproval
    73	
    74	`guide rule-recipes scheduled-reapproval` keeps editable drafts private and
    75	copies the approved revision into an independent public table when due.
    76	Editing or withdrawing atomically deletes the public copy and clears approval.
    77	Reapproval is explicit, even for the same revision after withdrawal. The job
    78	cannot republish a withdrawn revision. Scheduled runs coalesce; publication
    79	happens at the next job run at or after publishAt, rather than at an exact
    80	wall-clock instant (`guide jobs`).
    81	
    82	```quarry
    83	use personas alice, bob, carol
    84	table scheduledDrafts:
    85	  owner: principal
    86	  body: text 1..200
    87	  revision: int default 1
    88	  approved: bool default false
    89	  publishAt: time
    90	  immutable owner
    91	  read <- .owner = viewer
    92	  insert <- .owner = viewer and .approved = false
    93	  update <- .owner = viewer
    94	  online only
    95	table scheduledPublications:
    96	  draft: scheduledDrafts
    97	  revision: int
    98	  body: text 1..200
    99	  unique draft
   100	  immutable draft
   101	  public 'approved revision bodies are public until withdrawal'
   102	  delete <- exists scheduledDrafts[.draft, owner = viewer]
   103	  online only
   104	fn retractScheduled(draft: scheduledDrafts):
   105	  p = scheduledPublications[draft = draft]
   106	  if p != null:
   107	    delete scheduledPublications[p.id]
   108	mutation newScheduledDraft(body: text 1..200, publishAt: time):
   109	  return insert scheduledDrafts { owner: viewer, body, publishAt }
   110	mutation editScheduledDraft(draft: scheduledDrafts, body: text 1..200):
   111	  d = scheduledDrafts[draft] ! else NOT_FOUND
   112	  retractScheduled(draft)
   113	  update scheduledDrafts[draft] { body, revision: d.revision + 1, approved: false }
   114	mutation approveScheduledDraft(draft: scheduledDrafts, publishAt: time):
   115	  retractScheduled(draft)
   116	  update scheduledDrafts[draft] { approved: true, publishAt }
   117	mutation withdrawScheduledDraft(draft: scheduledDrafts):
   118	  update scheduledDrafts[draft] { approved: false }
   119	  retractScheduled(draft)
   120	job publishScheduledDrafts(periodStart: time, periodEnd: time):
   121	  for d in scheduledDrafts:
   122	    if d.approved and d.publishAt <= periodEnd:
   123	      upsert scheduledPublications[draft = d.id] { draft: d.id, revision: d.revision, body: d.body }
   124	schedule scheduledPublisher every 1m: publishScheduledDrafts
   125	query scheduledPublication(draft: scheduledDrafts):
   126	  return scheduledPublications[draft = draft]
   127	query scheduledDraft(draft: scheduledDrafts):
   128	  return scheduledDrafts[draft]
   129	```
   130	
   131	Only the system job inserts/updates publications; the owner may delete a copy.
   132	The approved bit always describes the current draft revision because the only
   133	editing mutation clears it in the same transaction. Add that invariant to every
   134	future draft writer. Already downloaded public content cannot be recalled.
   135	
   136	## Private bookings with public availability counts
   137	
   138	`guide rule-recipes booking-counts` discloses occupancy while keeping names
   139	private. A maintained view sees all bookings; a caller-filtered count would
   140	miss other people's reservations and oversell. Its public count deliberately
   141	reveals even one-person join/leave activity. It is not anonymity against timing
   142	inference. Empty authorized groups contribute zero.
   143	
   144	```quarry
   145	use personas alice, bob, carol
   146	table availabilitySlots:
   147	  capacity: int 1..20
   148	  immutable capacity
   149	  public 'slot capacity and occupied count are public'
   150	  online only
   151	table privateBookings:
   152	  slot: availabilitySlots
   153	  principal: principal
   154	  unique slot, principal else ALREADY_BOOKED
   155	  immutable slot, principal
   156	  read <- .principal = viewer
   157	  insert <- .principal = viewer
   158	  delete <- .principal = viewer
   159	  online only
   160	view bookedCount: count privateBookings by .slot
   161	  read <- allow
   162	  disclosure reviewed 'public occupancy reveals reservation and cancellation deltas'
   163	mutation reserveAvailable(slot: availabilitySlots):
   164	  s = availabilitySlots[slot] ! else NOT_FOUND
   165	  require bookedCount[slot] < s.capacity else SLOT_FULL
   166	  return insert privateBookings { slot, principal: viewer }
   167	mutation cancelAvailable(slot: availabilitySlots):
   168	  delete privateBookings[slot, viewer]
   169	query availability(slot: availabilitySlots):
   170	  s = availabilitySlots[slot] ! else NOT_FOUND
   171	  return { capacity: s.capacity, booked: bookedCount[slot], free: s.capacity - bookedCount[slot] }
   172	query myBooking(slot: availabilitySlots):
   173	  return privateBookings[slot, viewer]
   174	```
   175	
   176	Admission and insertion run in one writer transaction. Keep all booking writers
   177	behind this capacity check, including future service paths. Capacity is
   178	immutable so reducing it cannot silently invalidate existing bookings.
   179	
   180	## Privacy-aware leaderboard
   181	
   182	`guide rule-recipes private-leaderboard` publishes only an opted-in owner's
   183	chosen display name and exact total. Attempts stay private. A maintained own
   184	total includes every attempt; the only attempt writer refreshes an existing
   185	public entry in the same transaction. Opting out deletes that entry. The
   186	leaderboard cannot infer a nonparticipant's score by subtracting a global total.
   187	
   188	```quarry
   189	use personas alice, bob, carol
   190	table privateAttempts:
   191	  owner: principal
   192	  points: int 0..100
   193	  immutable owner, points
   194	  read <- .owner = viewer
   195	  insert <- .owner = viewer
   196	  online only
   197	view ownAttemptTotal: sum privateAttempts of .points by .owner
   198	  read <- .owner = viewer
   199	table publicLeaders:
   200	  owner: principal
   201	  name: text 1..40
   202	  points: int
   203	  unique owner
   204	  immutable owner
   205	  public 'opted-in display names, principals, exact totals and rank are public'
   206	  insert <- .owner = viewer
   207	  update <- .owner = viewer
   208	  delete <- .owner = viewer
   209	  online only
   210	mutation recordAttempt(points: int 0..100):
   211	  insert privateAttempts { owner: viewer, points }
   212	  entry = publicLeaders[owner = viewer]
   213	  if entry != null:
   214	    update publicLeaders[entry.id] { points: ownAttemptTotal[viewer] }
   215	mutation joinLeaderboard(name: text 1..40):
   216	  return upsert publicLeaders[owner = viewer] { owner: viewer, name, points: ownAttemptTotal[viewer] }
   217	mutation leaveLeaderboard():
   218	  delete publicLeaders[owner = viewer]
   219	query leaderboard(c: cursor ?):
   220	  return publicLeaders last 20 by .points, .id after c { name, points }
   221	query myAttemptTotal():
   222	  return ownAttemptTotal[viewer]
   223	```
   224	
   225	This consent reveals score deltas and relative ordering, even in a small group;
   226	principals remain public in the stored rows despite the query's projection.
   227	Use a separate unlinked publication model if principal privacy is required.
   228	Any future attempt edit/delete must refresh the public entry too. Never keep a
   229	public aggregate over all private attempts as a shortcut.
   230	
   231	## Mutable ancestor access across three levels
   232	
   233	`guide rule-recipes three-level-live` uses one ordinary authority root at every
   234	depth: project → folder → file. Folder and file each directly inherit the
   235	project's whole live rule, with separate per-project product caps. The copied
   236	project reference is immutable routing, not a copied mutable owner. The file
   237	insert rule validates that its folder belongs to that project. Transferring the
   238	project changes all existing folders and files atomically. No application
   239	maintenance loop is required.
   240	
   241	```quarry
   242	use personas alice, bob, carol
   243	table ancestorProjects:
   244	  owner: principal
   245	  read <- .owner = viewer or viewer = 'service:ops'
   246	  update <- viewer = 'service:ops'
   247	  online only
   248	table ancestorFolders:
   249	  project: ancestorProjects
   250	  immutable project
   251	  cap 5 by project
   252	  read through live .project
   253	  insert <- allow
   254	  online only
   255	table ancestorFiles:
   256	  folder: ancestorFolders
   257	  project: ancestorProjects
   258	  body: text 1..100
   259	  immutable folder, project
   260	  cap 20 by project
   261	  read through live .project
   262	  insert <- exists ancestorFolders[.folder, project = .project]
   263	  online only
   264	mutation addAncestorFolder(project: ancestorProjects):
   265	  return insert ancestorFolders { project }
   266	mutation addAncestorFile(folder: ancestorFolders, body: text 1..100):
   267	  f = ancestorFolders[folder] ! else NOT_FOUND
   268	  return insert ancestorFiles { folder, project: f.project, body }
   269	mutation transferAncestor(project: ancestorProjects, owner: principal):
   270	  require viewer = 'service:ops' else OPERATOR_ONLY
   271	  update ancestorProjects[project] { owner }
   272	query ancestorFile(file: ancestorFiles):
   273	  return ancestorFiles[file]
   274	query ancestorFolder(folder: ancestorFolders):
   275	  return ancestorFolders[folder]
   276	```
   277	
   278	LIVE's first form refuses chained `read through live .folder` when the folder
   279	itself inherits a rule. Flatten the authority root as above when all levels
   280	have identical readers. The 077 proposal workflow has additional per-section
   281	owner grants; inheriting only its proposal would widen access. Keep its stable
   282	proposal/section routing columns and exact online keyed probes for those
   283	different policies, validating correspondence on every insert. Bare captured
   284	`read through` cannot follow a mutable state flag. A mutable audience copy is
   285	still appropriate for unsupported live compositions, such as timed parents,
   286	or when the child intentionally has a different audience; maintain it
   287	atomically through every parent writer (`current-roster`, Manual copy
   288	alternative). An immutable audience copy instead preserves creation-time access.
   289	
   290	The 085 hymn entries share their service's complete rule and can use
   291	`read through live .service` online after removing the writing cascade action
   292	from that endpoint; delete the capped entries explicitly before their service.
   293	Its public-or-staff rule stays online. Keeping cascade semantics unchanged
   294	continues to require the existing explicit keyed probes. LIVE never silently
   295	drops a reader branch or a reference action.
   296	
   297	## Versioned status transitions with private history
   298	
   299	`guide rule-recipes private-history` uses an expected version to reject a stale
   300	transition, a named old/next rule for the permitted edge, and an append-only
   301	owner-readable history row in the same transaction. No public status/history
   302	aggregate leaks the private transition. A hidden task is `NOT_FOUND` before
   303	the program's `STALE_VERSION` or `BAD_TRANSITION` checks.
   304	
   305	```quarry
   306	use personas alice, bob, carol
   307	table versionedTasks:
   308	  owner: principal
   309	  status: enum('draft', 'submitted', 'approved') default 'draft'
   310	  version: int default 1
   311	  immutable owner
   312	  read <- .owner = viewer
   313	  insert <- .owner = viewer and .status = 'draft' and .version = 1
   314	  update (old, next) <- old.owner = viewer and next.owner = old.owner and ((old.status = 'draft' and next.status = 'submitted') or (old.status = 'submitted' and next.status = 'approved'))
   315	  online only
   316	table privateStatusHistory:
   317	  task: versionedTasks
   318	  owner: principal
   319	  version: int
   320	  status: enum('draft', 'submitted', 'approved')
   321	  at: time default now
   322	  unique task, version
   323	  immutable task, owner, version, status, at
   324	  read <- .owner = viewer
   325	  insert <- .owner = viewer and exists versionedTasks[.task, owner = .owner, version = .version, status = .status]
   326	  online only
   327	mutation newVersionedTask():
   328	  return insert versionedTasks { owner: viewer }
   329	mutation transitionTask(task: versionedTasks, expected: int, status: enum('draft', 'submitted', 'approved')):
   330	  t = versionedTasks[task] ! else NOT_FOUND
   331	  require t.version = expected else STALE_VERSION
   332	  require (t.status = 'draft' and status = 'submitted') or (t.status = 'submitted' and status = 'approved') else BAD_TRANSITION
   333	  update versionedTasks[task] { status, version: t.version + 1 }
   334	  insert privateStatusHistory { task, owner: viewer, version: t.version + 1, status }
   335	query versionedTask(task: versionedTasks):
   336	  return versionedTasks[task]
   337	query statusHistory(task: versionedTasks, c: cursor ?):
   338	  return privateStatusHistory where .task = task and .owner = viewer first 20 by .version after c
   339	```
   340	
   341	The expected-version check and increment are application invariants in this
   342	mutation; the table transition rule independently authorizes the status edge.
   343	Keep every future writer on the same version/history path. History insert
   344	failure rolls the status update back. `LANG4` owns the query-audience and
   345	`N_REQUIRE_REFUSED` diagnostics for these workflows.

## 13. Wave 15 authorization recipes (RULES5)

This amendment specifies applications of existing authorization semantics on
LIVE, rather than extending the language or relaxing a rule. It changes no
planner, protocol, adoption, ids, expiry, client contract or engine ceilings.
The commission is wave-15 fix list #4 excluding its two LANG4 note bullets.

### 13.1 Refusal ladder and probes

The rules card executes three standalone schemas: an asserted hidden lookup
refuses NOT_FOUND before a later state requirement; a hidden secondary unique
conflict suppresses even an authored `else` code and returns E_RULE; and a
rule `exists` can admit a write using a row that a caller-filtered program
`exists` cannot read. Statement order and rollback still govern, not a new
universal phase order. Successful blind writes can intentionally disclose that
their rule held. No hidden conflict key, clause or row enters the public refusal.
The exact program-probe teaching block intentionally emits one
N_UNREADABLE_PROBE; compile and packed tests pin its code and site. It teaches
caller-visible existence, never global absence enforcement.

### 13.2 Recipe invariants

- Readable totals pin the caller's owner scope. They return the complete domain
  total or unavailable; they never expose a subtotal as a complete total.
- Durable handoffs use server `connected` in the mutation and independently
  enforce enrolled-recipient/sender rules. Poll completion retains the existing
  35-second grace. Acceptance during that window is intentional and does not
  assert receipt or future connectivity. Offline calls queue without prediction.
  Ephemeral tables cannot grant durable authority (LLP 3000.017).
- Scheduled publication stores private current draft revision plus approval;
  edits and withdrawal retract the public copy atomically. Editing clears
  approval. Reapproval retracts any previous copy before choosing a new due
  time. The system's stable-id sweep publishes only approved due revisions;
  system jobs bypass source rules, so approval is checked in that job. Ordinary
  callers have no public-copy insert/update rule. Coalescing publishes at the
  next due run, with no exact-instant or downloaded-byte recall claim.
- Private bookings expose a reviewed maintained count, including empty-group
  zero, rather than a caller-filtered capacity count. Admission and insert are
  one writer transaction. All future booking writers must share the check;
  immutable capacity prevents silent shrinking below occupancy. Public count
  deltas are an intentional disclosure, not protection from timing inference.
- The leaderboard keeps attempts private and publishes exact totals only for
  opted-in owners. Every attempt writer refreshes an existing entry atomically;
  opting out deletes it. Names, principals, totals, deltas and relative rank
  become public by consent. Projection alone does not hide source columns.
- Versioned transitions first read the private task, then check expected version
  and the permitted status edge, update and append private history atomically.
  A named old/next rule independently enforces the status edge. Version/history
  invariants belong to every future writer; a failed history insert rolls back
  the update. There is no new generic compare-and-swap or automatic history.

### 13.3 Three levels and LIVE admission

LLP 3000.014.000 remains the authority: complete current-parent rule, immutable
required direct reference, exact product cap and bounded atomic maintenance.
The three-level recipe flattens the authority root onto each level, so the
folder and file are separate direct live children of the ordinary project.
The file's immutable project reference is checked against its folder at insert.
It is a hierarchy routing copy, not mutable copied authority. Project transfer
updates both live-child families in one transaction. This is valid only when
all levels share exactly that root's readers.

A chain of live children remains refused. Wave-15 lane 077 has additional
section-owner grants, so a proposal-only inherited rule would widen access.
Its stable copied ancestor ids plus exact online probes remain appropriate
for those distinct policies. Captured read-through cannot track mutable state.
Lane 085's entries can inherit the complete service rule online once its
writing cascade action is removed and capped explicit child deletion replaces
it. With cascade retained the live composition stays refused; no reader branch
or reference action may silently disappear. Mutable audience copies remain the
atomic application-maintained choice for unsupported live compositions or a
different intended child audience; immutable copies preserve creation access.

### 13.4 Qualification and review

Every added Quarry block is extracted from the installed packed CLI and runs
through the packed client/native packages in the existing publication-rules
qualification path. Tests cover positive, hidden and refused cases, totals,
capacity, consent withdrawal, grace/reconnect, scheduled withdrawal/edit/
reapproval, ancestor transfer and stale/private history. Language guide checks
compile all blocks and allow only the one explicitly taught probe note.
The 077/085 original schemas and live candidates are surveyed read-only and
the precise supported/refused shapes are retained under reports/wave15-rules5.
Paired independent Muse and fresh Codex reviews and disposition are recorded
below when received. LANG4 owns query-audience and N_REQUIRE_REFUSED notes.

# LLP 3000.014.000: Live parent authority

**Type:** RFC
**Status:** Implemented (2026-10-17; rev5 paired READY, implementation commissioned by the author; target 0.4.32)
**Systems:** Snapback 4 — Quarry read rules, completeness, partition admission, invalidation, devices, adoption
**Author:** Codex for Charlie Cheever
**Date:** 2026-10-16
**Revised:** 2026-10-17 (revision 5: bounded lifecycle collectors and restoration identities)
**Related:** [LLP 3000.014](./3000.014-a-child-reads-through-its-parent.rfc.md), [LLP 3000.036.000.000](./3000.036.000.000-union-sync-audiences.rfc.md), [LLP 3000.039](./3000.039-a-reference-declares-what-deleting-its-row-does.rfc.md)

## 1. Decision and review authority

Wave 14 lanes 007, 032 and 085 ask for children readable by their parent's
current readers, including when its department or assigned principal changes.
The language supports `read through live .parent`, the child's whole read rule, implemented
by compiler-owned copies maintained atomically on every parent policy write.
Unlike captured `read through`, policy inputs may change. Unlike the manual
recipe, builders neither declare the copies nor implement transfer loops.

This revision replaces **all normative text** of revisions 1 and 2. Those
revisions remain in git and their reviews remain linked in §10. There is no
parent-keyed sync scope, authority manifest, global authority counter, staged
manifest swap, extra reverse membership traversal, or new expiry algorithm.
The bounded population is the set of child rows of one parent, declared by
the builder as an ordinary product constraint. Reject shapes whose bound or
transactional maintenance cannot be proved. Do not approximate the rule.

Revision 3 is retained at `3b6df35e59`; its paired NOT READY reviews are in §10.

The LIVE workstream commission on 2026-10-17 authorizes up to five additional
paired rounds (3–7), then implementation only after both reviewers mark the
same revision READY. If not converged, leave Review and ship no partial form.
The earlier RULES4 two-round fallback remains shipped and valid until then.

## 2. Surface and admission

Example (illustrative Quarry until implemented):

```quarry
table departments:
  public 'department names'
table members:
  department: departments
  principal: principal
  unique department, principal
  immutable department, principal
  read <- .principal = viewer
  insert <- allow
  delete <- .principal = viewer
table projects:
  department: departments
  read <- exists members[.department, viewer]
  insert <- allow
  update <- allow
  sync
table tasks:
  project: projects
  body: text <= 2000
  immutable project
  cap 20 by project
  read through live .project
  insert <- allow
  update <- allow
  sync
```

The parser represents a separate live-through declaration; it is not a rule
term and cannot appear under `not`, conjuncts, or a second read declaration.
The binder produces `Through.mode = Live { max_children: N }`; old serialized
Through has default Captured mode. It maps *every* focal parent read-rule
field to the same private typed engine field used by captures today. The
compiled read rule is the **whole** parent's rule with those substitutions,
including every disjunct, predicate, literal and service grant. Its map and
mode participate in Through.identity. No hidden field is author-writable,
query-visible, in `*`, or present in generated public types.

Admission (checked both by the language binder and core schema validator):

1. Reference is required, immutable, ordinary application-table typed and
   default-free. Child has `cap N by reference` with exactly that one key;
   take the smallest matching positive N. A whole-table cap is not accepted
   as a substitute in this revision. Parent and child have no time bounds,
   no ephemeral state, no engine-generated/maintained columns, no timelines,
   no row/aggregate views sourced from either endpoint, and no field owners.
2. Parent has no Through. No captured or live table may use a live table as
   its parent. Thus captured-over-captured stays unchanged; all live/captured
   mixed edges and live-over-live are refused. One parent may have multiple
   separately capped live child tables; prices sum across those schema edges.
3. Parent RuleExpr must use only Public/Allow/Deny, direct focal scalar fields,
   viewer/literals, Eq/Ne, And/Or/Not, and ordinary Exists with total unique
   keys. Refuse created predicates, live rule-hop terms, maintained/private/
   recorded inputs, nonordinary probe targets, not-before and expiry anywhere
   in the transitive read-probe graph. Compute the graph by visited table set,
   including cycles; inspect every reached table's time declarations and
   Through/ephemeral/derived classification. A non-time membership Exists is
   accepted. An identical membership target with `expire at` is refused.
4. Sync inference uses the *entire substituted rule* and existing exact
   audience admission. Whole service disjuncts are removed only by the
   existing service-audience recognizer. A single public, principal-column or
   membership audience syncs; a union or any other noninvertible parent rule
   requires an `online only` live child. A union-synced parent may have an
   online live child, as existing union validation permits, but cannot supply
   that child with sync. No union/Through admission is widened.
   Refuse delivered-history and horizons for a live child: use all-current,
   until-revoked sync. No service-only branch creates a device audience.
5. A parent may be online only even if a child's rule is invertible. Such
   children sync via their copies as captured children do; device parent-
   pinned completeness may need server fallback if the parent is not held.

### 2.1 Closed predicate and referential-action boundary

The core validator, not only the binder, checks the following closed predicate.
Admissible rule nodes are `Allow`, `Deny`, `Public { reason }`, `Eq`, `Ne`,
`And`, `Or`, `Not`, `Exists`. Admissible terms are `Viewer`, `Literal` and
`Field` naming id or a declared ordinary scalar column; inspect every key and
extra equality term. Refuse `Reference`, `ReferenceChain`, old/next-image and
other term forms. Scalars are required/optional Id/Principal/Ref/Bool/Int/
IntRange/Time/Decimal/Money/Enum/Text/Bytes; refuse List/Json/asset terms. A
literal must have the binder-proved comparable type. Public and Allow both
have zero mapped fields. A total unique key has every nonoptional key column
pinned, or has a same-term non-null proof for each optional key argument;
A non-null proof is exactly Viewer, a non-null Literal, or Field of a required
column, and nothing else; guarded optional fields are refused for this initial
live admission rather than introducing a binder-only proof. The binder and
core apply this identical structural predicate. Fully pinned supersets keep
all extra equality checks. Core validation uses
its existing unique-probe admission, not a new interpretation of positional
arguments. Created and unsupported variants refuse before substitution.

For endpoints, the closed refused declaration set is `not_before`, `expire`,
`ephemeral`, `recorded`, `maintained`, `written_by`, `sequences`, any timeline
ownership, non-visible generated table, maintain target/source/row-key source,
and parent Through. Child Through is the one being checked. Its live reference
must use `on delete restrict` (the default). Every other authored reference on
both endpoints must have a non-writing action: Restrict or Keep. If any endpoint
`on_delete` entry is Cascade/SetNull, refuse with the field's `on delete restrict`
rewrite. This prohibits *all indirect reference-action writes into an endpoint*:
reference actions write the referencing table, and an action path reaching an
endpoint must end in such a prohibited declaration. Unrelated action graphs
remain unchanged. There is no new live-price composition through those graphs.

For every table reached via a read-rule Exists (including cycles, visited once),
refuse time bounds, Through, ephemeral/generated/derived/recorded/private-field
state by the same declaration checks; sequences on probe targets are harmless
but still refused initially. Probe indexes must be ordinary total unique keys.
Probe target read rules do not grant authority to a bare Exists, but scanning
them for this conservative transitive exclusion is intentional. Probe horizons
and leave policy are not time authority; their current device coverage rules
still apply. An absent witness cannot become true through negation unless its
absence is covered, as in the existing rule evaluator.

Child indexes may only be plain column indexes. After generation, prove each
encoded key's schema maximum (including id and separators) <=
`rows::MAX_INDEX_KEY_BYTES`; reject word/computed/contribution indexes and
unbounded/oversized key shapes, with a smaller scalar-bound or online manual
recipe rewrite. This proof applies to the hidden audience indexes as well.
Activation refuses grandfathered oversized stored keys. No policy patch can
then fail only because a hidden child forms an oversized combination.
Neither endpoint may serve as the target table of any sync audience: this
removes membership-delivery fanout from private maintenance; ordinary probe
rosters remain valid and use existing group delivery. Multiple child tables
for one parent are admitted, each independently capped and priced.
Refuse either endpoint in **any role** of a ReceiptFamily (scope/counter,
messages, members, runs or receipt source/marker), including inferred receipt
families. Audit the family's complete table-role list, not just scope. This
prevents commit_receipt_counter and allocation/marker maintenance from writing
an endpoint without a source reservation. Ordinary rule-probe rosters that
participate in no receipt family remain admitted. The refusal names the family
and endpoint role and links the manual current-roster recipe.

Accepted/refused pairs: Public parent with no focal fields / Public parent
with an expiry; nonexpiring `exists members[.department,viewer]` / same rule
with expiring members; full owner OR support rule online / that union on a
synced live child; ordinary Restrict reference / SetNull reference on the
parent; direct Field / ReferenceChain; one live edge / live parent used as a
captured parent. All refusals preserve the authored whole-rule meaning.

Use E_READ_THROUGH for unsupported shape, with the offending declaration and
an executable rewrite: add `cap N by reference` (a product limit chosen by the
builder); or keep captured semantics with immutable policy; or use the manual
current-roster transfer recipe; or online live keyed probes for rules those
probes can express. Never silently choose a cap or drop part of the rule.
`check` prints the declared cap, full fixed policy-write price and server-only
prediction fence, including the fact that a very large cap may make parent
writes exceed unchanged transaction ceilings even when no children exist.

## 3. Invariant and lifetime

For every active stored live child c with parent p:

- p exists in the same store;
- c.reference is p.id, and every private mapped field equals p's current
  corresponding field, with its declared type (null remains a value);
- the number of stored children for each (child table, reference) is <= N.

Since endpoint tables cannot expire or open later, the active physical child
population equals the ordinary cap population. Dormant rows are not active
and do not contribute until restored; restoring must revalidate the invariant
and cap before publication. No sweep or mutable inherited deadline is needed.
Child copies of deadline fields remain null, using the existing capture row
layout; unrelated ordinary/captured tables retain their current lifetime rules.

Use captured-through's existing physical-child restriction for parent delete,
including parent read authorization before occupancy. Parent id reuse remains
blocked while any physical child exists. Do not permit cascade or set-null on
the live reference, and do not weaken existing restrictions on ordinary refs.
The normal child cap is enforced on insert, upsert insert arm, insert-if-absent,
seed/import, system writes, adoption, dormant restoration and trusted restore.
A direct child update cannot change its parent or its private policy fields.

The invariant makes read-rule evaluation over copies exactly equivalent to
reading the current parent fields, for each committed server state and after
each complete language statement in a writing transaction. Exists probes stay
live (no membership copies). A membership revoke changes no child image and
uses the existing membership rule dependency and partition invalidation path.

## 4. Atomic maintenance algorithm

The binder generates a `$liveChildren` ordinary index `[reference, id]`,
distinct from the existing lifetime index. The core validator requires its
exact plain, nonunique shape, along with the cap and hidden-key maximum proof.
It is created/rebuilt before accepting writes.
Compile reverse edges once per schema: parent table -> sorted list of
(child table, reference, input map, N). This is schema-sized metadata only.

Before *any* parent target lookup/arm selection, reserve the sum of all child
edges' maximum maintenance prices, regardless of old row existence, policy
values, whether the patch mentions policy, or actual child counts. Reserve
once per source write attempt, not once per transaction (several writes to
the same parent may occur in one mutation). Upsert prepays both arms once.
All ordinary hidden-target meter restoration rules still apply.

After the authorized parent row/index write, and before the statement returns:

```
for edge in schema.reverse_live_edges[parent_table]:
    children = private_index_scan(edge, parent.id, limit=N+1)
    if children.len > N: abort entire transaction as store invariant failure
    for child in children:
        next = child with mapped engine inputs stamped from new_parent
        if next == child: continue
        replace image and all indexes using internal LiveAuthority origin
        row_changed(child, old, next, LiveAuthority)  # logs and invalidation
```

The hook is in the shared physical row-change tail, not a language operation
visitor. It covers direct update, both upsert arms, seed/import/system/restore
writers and no reference-action replacements (refused by §2.1). Delete takes the existing child
restriction rather than copying from None. Child insertion stamps current
parent inputs and requires a live readable parent, plus its own insert rule.
The transaction's uncommitted parent image is visible to that stamp. A child
inserted before a policy edit is included by the reverse index; one after it
stamps the new fields. Every subsequent program read observes the maintained
state, and any later failure rolls back rows, indexes, logs and metadata.

LiveAuthority origin may change only mapped engine inputs. It neither invokes
child authored update authority nor grants that authority to the caller. It
validates the F replaced scalars against the parent/child type map, preserving
every other validated cell and all authored constraints (which cannot name
engine fields). It replaces indexes and preserves caps, asset placements,
row-change logs and written-table dependencies. It does not run a whole-row
constraint/probe walk over unchanged private cells. No derived-data
recursion is admitted in §2. Only the core can mint this origin; client-supplied
engine fields continue to refuse before lookup. The parent write's own table
rules remain mandatory, including blind authorized writes as currently allowed.

### 4.1 Authoritative writer inventory

| Entry | Enforcement before commit |
| --- | --- |
| Direct update, upsert update, jobs/system language writes | target-attempt fixed prepayment; shared row_changed maintenance |
| Insert, insert-if-absent, upsert insert | same target-attempt prepayment for a parent; capture_insert stamps a child from this transaction's parent |
| Engine::seed/seed_at, seed_server_rows, Postgres seed | ordinary upsert path and stamp/maintenance; postbatch invariant validation |
| Engine::load Import | any Through schema routes through seed; cannot supply engine authority fields |
| Engine::load_stored_rows Restore, Postgres::load_trusted | separate bulk barrier: strip imported live inputs, install all endpoint rows privately, recalculate child inputs from installed parents, validate cap/references/indexes, then regenerate affected partition events from final images and commit |
| Activation, rollback, dormant restore | §7 before-projection/reverse-correspondence barrier and bounded rewriter; no ordinary writer-tail assumption |
| Reference-action endpoint replacement/deletion | statically refused by §2.1; a forged direct schema also refuses |
| Authoritative memory host seed/restore | seed_server_rows uses the same whole-call job account; restore_server_images delegates to the same bounded final durable-image barrier as native stores, including recapture and affected-group validation; source changes needing derivation maintenance use seed, not trusted-image restore |
| Device imported persistent state | store identity/generation and capability checks, trusted only at existing device persistence boundary; never server authority |

Restore installs a *whole consistent image* or rejects: it cannot partially
restore a parent's old policy while leaving existing children trusted. The
bulk barrier also scans existing children not present in the restore input;
recaptures all from the final parent. It preserves historical non-live
attribution but never trusts imported live copies. Do not emit intermediate
put/del events before this validation. The retained-state account is §4.2;
there is no assumption that PendingEvents is a streaming data structure.

### 4.2 Bounded bulk-call account

For schemas containing a live edge, every server seed/import/trusted-load call
uses **one** `Ceilings::job()` Abort meter for its entire atomic input/maintenance
transaction, with ordinary source and fixed live-edge reservations. Do not use
an unbounded meter or reset it between rows/pages. An iterator is consumed only
through byte-admitted pages; before `ancestor_order`/seed_server_rows could
collect a batch Vec, reserve its serialized images, ids, deep decoded copies,
ordering slots and vector capacity against this same account. A too-large
batch returns E_BOUND and commits nothing. The cap is the existing job envelope
(8192 writes, 16384 examinations, 16 MiB heap and other job dimensions), not a
new or raised ceiling. No claim that a whole arbitrary backup fits one call.

Preserve the earliest before-image or before-audience for every distinct input
identity, *and* every existing child that may be recaptured, before installing
its first replacement. Admission includes the identity map's key/capacity,
source image D, all retained PendingEvents slots (including withdrawn slots),
row/grant position vectors and born/touched/created maps. Use a bounded collector
wrapper that reserves before every growth, and shares the bulk meter; an
allocation failure/limit aborts with E_BOUND before memory is owned. Slots
already covered by held source/edge event reservations spend those credits
instead of charging twice; the wrapper accounts any uncovered allocation
before growth, and never releases credits for still-retained storage. Coalescing
identities are keyed (table,id); repeated images retain one original boundary,
but each retained slot and temporary input copy is still charged.

After full invariant validation, regenerate only final boundary events under
the same account. Before PendingEvents::take and partition::append, reserve
the dense event vector and whole-call append/coalescing-map peak in addition
to the still-resident collector. These allocations are not free merely
because images share Arc. Compute owned image/map/key/Vec allocations with
the existing internal heap helpers before growth; retain the charge until
append consumes/drops them. Event ordinals continue monotonically across the
one transaction and preserve ordinary birth/grant/withdrawal order. A callback
page's transient heap can release; any before-image, pending event, identity or
append slot that outlives it cannot. The bounded account includes finalization,
so a 100,000-parent restore fails atomically well before retaining 100,000
boundaries/events even though every per-parent child cap is one.

For SQLite and Postgres this is the same core BulkLedger/BulkCollector around
their respective seed/load entry points and common PendingEvents/append flow;
each store still preflights its own native row/key/statement scratch. No SQL
transaction is committed early. The operator rewrite is to split the input
into coherent bounded parent-plus-children batches (each final batch passes
all reference/copy invariants), or restore an offline database image and run
ordinary destination adoption before serving. Do not suggest chunking a single
parent's transfer: if that cap's fixed write price cannot fit, it remains a
single atomic refusal. Trusting an offline database image does not waive the
ordinary opening/version/identity/adoption validation.

## 5. Reservation and accounting contract

Use the seven existing Price dimensions; ceilings do not rise. The new helper
`schema::live::edge_price` is shared by full-schema compiler/server validation;
the device only consumes the exported price. Definitions for one child edge:

- N: declared cap; F: mapped scalar count; C: stored column count.
- D: `rows::disclosed::decode_reservation(child)`; unlike row_copy_ceiling,
  this includes private decode/serialized scratch, nested values and asset
  facts. P is the same bound for the parent, prepaid by the source writer.
- K = `MAX_INDEX_KEY_BYTES`; for each plain index i with width w_i define
  `B_i = 4*K + 128*(w_i+1) + 512`. This covers an owned encoded buffer,
  geometric growth, owned scalar components (bounded by the proved encoded
  key), per-Value slots, and index vector headers. Let B=sum(B_i), W=sum(w_i),
  I=index count. All indexes, including unchanged ones, are counted.
- A is 0 if no child sync, otherwise 1 (unions are refused). L is the larger
  of the child and parent table-name byte lengths, each also bounded by the
  portable index-label gate. M=`1024+4*K+4*L` bounds partition strings, row id,
  set/map entries and event metadata. E=`2*A*D + (2*A+4)*M` is retained event
  and per-row commit-bookkeeping storage, including born/touched/created maps.
  No child table may be an audience target (§2.1), hence no dependent joins.

Define the full edge reservation:

```
steps        = (N+1) * (64 + 8*(F+C+W) + 4*A)
index_entries= 2*N*I
examined     = N+1
rows_written = N
heap         = (N+1)*(D+K+256) + 4*D + 2*B + N*E
output       = 0
rule_probes  = 0
```

The scan retains at most N+1 private decoded images plus keys/vector slots;
the singleton sentinel diagnoses corruption. Replacement needs old/new map
copy-on-write and serialization scratch (4D); `index_write` simultaneously
holds all old/new key sets (2B), even unchanged indexes. Event collector
images/bookkeeping survive the write attempt (N*E). The constant 64 covers
scan/loop/write/row_changed bookkeeping; each scalar stamp, map cell copy and
key component gets up to eight logical steps; four per audience for old/new
partition handling. No recursive rule or derived-data walk runs under this
origin, so neither extra probes nor output are priced. Asset placement is
unchanged and does not walk the upload ledger. The parent image and public
result remain paid by ordinary source-write admission, not by this edge.

Extract event metadata construction from `schema::reservations::reservation`
(the existing inline ActionEvents calculation) into shared
`schema::reservations::event_envelope`; the live helper uses the explicit
D/M bound above and checks it is at least that helper's envelope. This names
a new extraction, not an existing exported function. Fixed source prices sum
all live edges using checked arithmetic; overflow refuses schema admission.
Missing projected live prices are a device prediction refusal, never zero.

Charge every edge's full non-heap dimensions and heap reservation before parent
lookup, including no-op writes and empty populations. N*E remains reserved
until the request ends; transient scan/index/copy heap releases after the
statement. Internal LiveAuthority work runs with a separate **bounded**
`Meter::new(edge_price ceilings, Abort)` and request physical allowance;
never `Ceilings::unbounded`. Its actual consumption is private. All index,
step, row-change and partition charging routes to that meter for this origin,
including the existing `origin == Maintained` charge branches. Neither double
charge the public meter nor silently skip physical accounting. Prepay/draw
N+1 physical child examinations before the first scan; native serialized
scratch is checked under D before private materialization. If this ledger
exhausts despite a valid invariant, abort generically and report an internal
reservation bug; never expose actual child work in a public Bound.

Bounded-ledger exhaustion is a design failure to fix before qualification,
not accepted ordinary product behavior. The price above is deliberately
conservative; check prints it even when it makes a large declared cap
impractical. Boundary tests independently count operations/retained bytes,
including many narrow indexes, maximum scalar keys, assets and nested payloads.
Parent update prediction is fenced via separate `live.server_execution=true`;
do not overload existing `server_only` (which means no fixed action price).
No indirect action can write an endpoint, and the direct root parent delete
keeps its ordinary reservation plus one live attempt reservation (a fixed prepayment for all attempt shapes,
including delete; the maintenance hook itself never runs for new=None).
Delete then runs the ordinary read-authorized physical-child restriction, not
a copy loop; it never enumerates policy maintenance through an action closure. The user's chosen
cap is not inferred from any private row population.

## 6. Completeness, invalidation and device contract

Server planner uses a distinct live uniformity witness: parent-pinned pages
resolve parent authorization once as today, then rely on §3, never on
immutable-input validation. Ordinary non-parent-pinned filtering and caps stay
unchanged. Completion still needs child range/horizon coverage. A denied or
absent parent yields known empty only on the server or with device coverage;
unknown remains unknown. Aggregates sourced from either endpoint are refused
by §2 until separately designed. Assets always use the child's complete read
rule and cannot bypass current authority through a cached placement.

Both endpoint tables and every probed table are dependencies of live child
reads. Each maintenance write marks the child table written; even empty-child
transitions mark it conservatively so subscriptions reconsider empty answers.
Use existing bounded subscriber scheduling after commit (no callback loop
inside the parent transaction). Watchers reevaluate on generation changes,
parent/roster writes and device coverage publication. Private evidence stays
host-only and no hidden parent fields enter cursors or public refusals.

Sync uses ordinary audience partitions and row moves: old copy -> new copy
produces the existing remove/put sequence at one transaction commit sequence.
Roster changes use existing group join/scope handling, independent of parent
or child count. There are no authority descriptors or extra per-parent empty
scope objects. Initial acquisition is the existing snapshot plus log catch-up;
its watermark also covers concurrent copy maintenance. A grant cannot mark a
snapshot complete before ordinary catch-up reaches its image watermark.

Add required device capability `live-parent-copy-v1` before schema/data decode.
Backend projections carry mode/map/cap, indexes and source reservation even
when the parent is online only. Older devices reject via E_DEVICE_VERSION.
Parent writers with any live edges are server-only predictions, with a
schema-level fence exported before private details are projected. A mutation
that might touch such a parent queues wholly, with no partial predictions.
Child-only mutations retain existing captured-through prediction proofs,
current parent requirement, unique/cap coverage and unchanged reservation.

On devices, copied fields are the authority at the last fully applied sync
state, as with ordinary audience-bearing rows. A parent-pinned shortcut must
run the existing `captures_match` test over all held current children and
check child audience coverage before borrowing the parent's verdict. A mismatch
is Unknown/server fallback, never denial or a complete empty answer. Read
paths that do not use the shortcut evaluate the complete substituted rule.
The coverage invariant is: a stream cursor advances only over an indivisible
commit prefix (partition::stream cut-by-seq); apply commits events and coverage
in one device transaction; snapshot::stage holds all acquisition pages private
until catch-up reaches max(SNAPSHOT_IMAGES_AT); publication precedes watcher
notification. Ordinary puts may be reread at images_at beyond the cursor, as
for all existing mutable multirow tables. Such an image cannot let a pinned
live proof borrow a mismatched parent verdict: captures_match scans held
children first; absent/unknown/mismatched authority yields Unknown. Unpinned
copy-rule reads use the same delivered-row contract as ordinary audience rows,
not a cross-table uniformity proof.

New envelope tests must prove that ordinary row-move delivery cannot publish
a mixed parent/child authorization proof as fresh: incremental pages, old/new roster
overlap, repeated transfers, disconnect/crash and duplicate/reorder.
If that property needs a new delivery mechanism, this design is NOT READY;
it must be designed and independently reviewed before implementation proceeds.

The availability argument is local: a parent edit reserves and finishes at
most sum(N) child updates in one transaction or rolls back; no polling scan
restarts. Unrelated parent or roster traffic never restarts acquisition beyond
the existing partition snapshot contract. There is no new global barrier.
Persistent ordinary resource exhaustion still returns the existing refusal.

## 7. Adoption and rollback

All mode/map/parent-rule changes are schema generations. Under the existing
exclusive activation transaction, pin time, apply predecessor lifetime
retirement and destination dormant projection, then validate *before* dropping
old authored audience columns. Use adaptive byte-admitted byId keyset pages through
store/activation_walk.rs::walk_metered (maximum 256 serialized rows), with
rows::adoption::Work including parent lookup, map rewriting and index entries.
Its callback scratch adds parent D, child D and 2B from §5, on top of the
still-resident page. page_fits shrinks the page before callbacks; a singleton
that cannot fit refuses before any write. Count/samples retain fixed schema
allowance (five bounded ids per table), not a row-sized list. Check every active child's parent exists; stamp the
expected destination inputs; check cap by the generated reference index;
check destination types and all ordinary constraints. Install rewritten rows,
indexes and generation/log reset atomically only after all validations pass.
An error, storage failure or process crash rolls back the activation;
no destination generation/grants may become visible early. No long-lived
cross-request staging or new activation worker is proposed.

For the shipped manual copy recipe, infer the previous-rule-to-parent-rule
field correspondence using the existing adoption `correspondence` algorithm
in engine/through_activation.rs (including whole service disjuncts). Require
the complete previous rule to match under that bijection and each surviving
child copy to equal its current parent's field before projection. This admits
the consistent copy-column recipe without id changes. An inconsistent recipe
refuses E_READ_THROUGH_ADOPT with operator-only count/samples and bridge-release
repair; it never silently grants new readers. Captured-to-live requires the
same value agreement and preserves ids. Live-to-captured requires destination
parent policy immutability; recapture and generation replacement are atomic.
The retained/historical-keep branch of activate_retained and through_activation
**never applies to a live-mode table**, including live-to-live cap edits and
restoration of dormant narrowed rows. Restamp every restored live row from
its current parent, ignoring old live inputs, then validate all caps/types;
a missing parent or any failed constraint refuses E_READ_THROUGH_ADOPT.
Captured-only historical semantics remain unchanged. Changing caps checks
current occupancy first; dormant restore applies these same live checks. For **live-to-manual**, including rollback and a dormant authored copy,
use the predecessor Through metadata before dropping it. Structurally map the
complete destination read rule to the current parent's rule, including all
service branches. For a matching map, overwrite each mapped destination
authored cell from the current parent (explicit language-owned reconciliation,
not a trust of dormant bytes), then validate destination constraints, indexes
and rules' full correspondence. Keep unrelated dormant authored cells intact.
If no complete bijection exists, refuse E_READ_THROUGH_ADOPT and give a bridge
release rewrite preserving the live rule until the manual policy is explicit.
Thus rolling A -> live -> B -> rollback cannot restore audience A. Schema
replacement with a deliberately different manual policy is a bridge release,
not a silent rollback inference. Rollback is a fresh destination activation.

Activation is linear total work with bounded working memory; the existing
operator lock-wait timeout is **not** an execution deadline, and a client
request disconnect does not cancel admitted work. This revision adds no new
activation cancellation contract. The owner reports running until the actual
transaction succeeds or fails; a network timeout means unknown outcome,
requiring generation/store inspection before retry. SQLite error/process death
rolls back an uncommitted activation. A long activation may hold the ordinary
writer lock; do not claim continuous old-store availability during it. No
new cross-request staging, resumable worker or mutation ceiling is introduced.
Restore uses the same byte-admitted validator and final-image barrier.

### 7.1 Dormant-restoration identities are a relation

For any transition whose previous or destination schema contains a live edge,
activate a transaction-local restoration sink **before** dormant projection.
Replace the activation_*_restored_ids Vec/JSON paths in dormant.rs (table and
column restoration), narrowed.rs and release_history::finalize_restorations
with SQL inserts/selects on `_live_restored(kind, table_num, column_name, id)`;
its composite primary key deduplicates identities. Empty column_name marks
a table/narrowed-row entry. Insert ids via INSERT SELECT before deleting the
source dormant rows/cells; narrowed restoration does the same before removing
its retained-row evidence. Per-row callbacks may also insert a single bounded
identity. No table-sized SELECT result or JSON id array is materialized.

At finalization, for each schema-sized (kind, table, column) declaration,
COUNT joined active r rows and publish the same scalar restoration counts as
existing metadata; at most five sample ids come from ORDER BY id LIMIT 5.
The existing release summary keeps its external counts/format. Clear old
activation_*_restored_ids metadata at transaction start for this path and do
not parse stale predecessor JSON arrays. Live-to-manual rollback is included
even though the destination has no live edge. Nonlive transitions retain their
existing path; this change does not claim to repair all unrelated lifecycle
memory behavior.

The relation is SQLite file-backed TEMP storage in the same database
transaction (temp_store=FILE for the owned activation connection, without
changing other connection policies); never an in-memory map. Its disk size is
O(restored identities), at most one record per restored (kind,table,column,id),
with bounded identity text and schema names. The ordinary storage capacity
and SQL errors bound persistence; disk-full rolls back the activation and
all identity inserts. Reserve one relation row plus limited query/cursor/page
scratch in the activation walker before Rust owns it. A connection-local table
is emptied at entry and before successful commit; rollback/crash leaves no
published restoration counts or live authority. No relation content travels
to clients. Postgres has no release dormant support; no PG dormant claim is
added. Its live bulk paths are bounded by §4.2 instead.

Tests must exercise many parents with cap one and payloads that force page
shrinking, late bulk-limit failures after actual rewrites, repeated identities,
all three restoration kinds, rollback into a manual destination and disk-full
failure. Assert bounded Rust-owned peak, ordinary scalar summary equality and
no partially committed row/index/log/generation state.

## 8. Required implementation and falsification

Implement only after paired READY. Layer tests: parser/invalid combinations;
core direct-schema admission (cannot forge mode, cap or price); full rule
substitution including OR-heavy lanes 007/085; fixed meters at zero/N children,
boundary exhaustion and hidden parent targets; insert/upsert/if-absent/system/
import/reference actions; same-transaction reads/rollback; parent-pinned and
unscoped completeness; ordinary and asset reads; held-watch invalidation;
parent deletion/id reuse; union refusal and service-branch sync; both device stores;
generation/capability refusal; snapshot transfer concurrent with acquisition;
stream paging/crash/reorder; adoption from exact manual guide recipe, bad
copies, dormant rows and bidirectional rollback.

Check guides execute with no surprise notes, retain the manual alternative,
and record 0.4.32 changes without bumping the worktree's 0.4.30 packages.
Run difftest run 20k, devices seeds 0–999 and Lean versus base signatures;
100-schema before/after; wasm measurement plus 500 B with reason; packed Linux
qualification; affected Mac Studio tests; root guide/inventory prerequisites
and ref-check. Preserve actual results and any limits in WORKSTREAM-REPORT.md.

## 9. Choice among alternatives

A live read hop alone is straightforward online but leaves delivery audience
inversion and sync catch-up unsolved. Revision 2's poll-time scopes created
unbounded manifest/staging objects and a global restart domain. A declared
child cap plus maintained copies fits existing row-move sync, preserves exact
authority and completion for accepted shapes, and puts transaction fanout under
a fixed visible product limit. An unbounded child population is deliberately
refused. This narrows accepted schemas, not the meaning of an accepted rule.
The guide must say this limit plainly and offer the manual alternative.

  eliminate those mechanisms; use generation-qualified ordinary row moves

  ordinary logs and existing device staging consume existing accounts (§6).

  transitive time check (§§2–3), no mutable derived deadlines or cascade.

  need coherent bounded input batches; per-parent transfers never split.

selected: both r4 reviews found the fixed-price ordinary loop structurally
sound, so preserving existing blind parent-write behavior is preferred.

and [fresh Codex](reviews/3000.014.000-live-parent-r5.codex.md) both READY on

design review claims an implementation or qualification result; the scoped

### Implementation proof clarifications

Ordinary constraint keys and unique keys on live children use authored cells;
mutable engine inputs cannot participate in those constraints. Nonunique
routing indexes still carry the engine inputs. The restoration identity relation
also holds a per-row live-source provenance kind until the entire barrier
finishes; it is not a durable grant and never enters device envelopes. This
prevents formerly-live images from taking captured-history or immediate-manual
shortcuts after intermediate generations. Destination decode/index scratch is
admitted alongside predecessor pages before any rewrite callback.

Dormant value cells persist their own live-source bit alongside the value.
Shared column definitions describe shape and cannot replace that per-cell
origin when rows from captured and live generations coexist. Restoring cells
copies the bit into the file-backed per-row activation relation before source
evidence deletion. Legacy captured-only stores add the bit with default false
inside the activation transaction; pruning and deletion remove it with its cell.

Row authority mode is independent of a dormant cell's origin. The activation
relation retains each restored row's actual manual/captured/live source mode;
column shape recovery cannot rewrite that mode in a narrowed-row definition.
A manual source with older live cells proves its immediate policy before a
captured or live destination may recapture. Original captured inputs/deadlines
retain their own history; an unused older live cell cannot make the entire row
live. Existing captured-definition bridge refusals remain unchanged.

The refreshable values of live policy inputs do not bind historical audience
reference lifetimes. The immutable parent reference and its deletion/reuse
receipt always do. Authored references and original captured inputs retain
ordinary dormant lifetime checks.


### Implementation and qualification record

Implemented through edcbd2e479 for the coordinator's 0.4.32 integration.
Design rev5 received paired READY in r5. Full implementation reviews and
successive correction reviews are retained verbatim under reviews/; the final
native adoption correction received paired READY in implementation r14
(scoped delta 94b32b3714..edcbd2e479). The per-point dispositions are in
snapback4/reports/wave14-live/implementation-review-responses.md.

The qualification bundle records source-bound Linux/Mac tests, packed
packages, the exact 100-schema survey, 20,000 ordinary differential cases,
device seeds 0–999 and 20,000 Lean cases. Lean retains exactly the baseline's
four known signatures; the inherited PostgreSQL fixture failure is separately
reproduced and ticketed. Later native-only dormant-adoption corrections are
qualified by affected tests and packed artifacts, without claiming that the
differential generators cover dormant release adoption. Web measurement and

## Summary

Snapback 4 is one of two parallel candidates for the successor to Snapback 2,
and the sibling of Snapback 3, not its successor. It builds
[LLP 2000.000](./2000.000-keep-the-core-flip-two-substrates.rfc.md) as
written: the Snapback 2 core kept verbatim, two substrates flipped up front —
resources metered at runtime instead of priced statically, and a client that
replicates the viewer's declared partition instead of caching receipted
results — proved first on the hub fixture, with a deterministic simulator as
the primary harness. Snapback 3 ([LLP 2000](./2000-snapback3.rfc.md), led by
Codex / GPT-6 Astra) keeps the same two questions open and discovers their
answers in its first slice. Snapback 2 ([LLP 1000](./1000-snapback2.explainer.md))
continues as the incumbent and the control. All three are judged by one
pre-registered comparison on one clock.

This document records the author's decisions of 2026-09-12 that created the
track, restates the bet on one page so a reader can orient without the brief,
fixes the bakeoff protocol, draws the fence, and plans the first four weeks.
The design argument and its evidence stay in LLP 2000.000. Only running code
will bind (`rules/RULES.md`).

## 1. Decisions (author, 2026-09-12)

On 2026-09-12 Charlie Cheever asked whether Claude Fable 5.1 should lead a
Snapback 4 while Codex / GPT-6 Astra leads Snapback 3 and Snapback 2
continues, "and see which one turns out best." The lead answered yes with
conditions; the author accepted them ("Sounds good") and asked for this
document. The decisions below are therefore the lead's proposal as accepted
by the author. Where the author has not separately ruled, the text says so.

1. **Three tracks, one comparison.** Snapback 2 continues as the incumbent
   and the control. Snapback 3 builds LLP 2000; Snapback 4 builds LLP
   2000.000. This is a bakeoff of *bets* — discover the substrates in the
   slice versus decide them first — not of models. The result will say more
   about the bets than about the leads, and §3 is written so that it can.
2. **The name is Snapback 4.** Siblings, not a sequence: "4" does not mean
   "after 3." A separate number gives the track its own corpus root,
   directory, package identity and data directory with no rename when the
   bakeoff resolves, and files never move in LLP. If Snapback 4 loses, it is
   a number that never shipped. "Snapback 3b" was considered and rejected:
   it reads as a variant of 3, and this is a different bet.
3. **Snapback 4 builds LLP 2000.000 as written.** Its §14 open questions are
   the lead's to decide inside the track, recorded in §7 as they are
   decided. The author rules on the bar, the comparison and promotion (§3).
4. **One owner on the core.** The lead writes the engine, the store and the
   client contract. Codex lanes build the fixture generator, the reference
   applications, the device witnesses and the periphery. Blind reviews from
   two model families are reserved for the two disclosure-sensitive
   mechanisms: partition inversion of read rules (LLP 2000.000 §7.1) and
   the meter's cut behaviour on non-uniform scans (LLP 2000.000 §6). This
   departs from the standing rule that Codex codes and Claude orchestrates,
   deliberately, for the core only.
5. **The clock is four weeks** from the first commit under `snapback4/` to
   the first slice running on the hub fixture. At the clock the author kills
   the track, merges its substrates into a survivor, or continues it. There
   is no third month without a ruling.
6. **Shared and not shared.** Shared with Snapback 3: the LLP corpus, the
   Clique harness and scorers, the fixtures, the briefs, and the simulator
   once either track has one. Not shared: code, except the Snapback 2 pieces
   both are expected to copy (LLP 2000.000 §5.1).
7. **Snapback 2 keeps shipping and freezes new substrate work for the
   duration.** Fix-forward, the loop bar, acquisition and papercuts continue;
   no new certificate axes, client custody mechanisms or ceiling changes.
   This is accepted here as a condition of the bakeoff. Making it binding on
   concurrent Snapback 2 sessions needs one line in `rules/NOT-DOING.md`'s
   Moves ledger, which this document does not write.
8. **Reading load.** This root, updated in place, and one append-only
   journal, `snapback4/JOURNAL.md`. Sub-LLPs only for a decision that must
   outlive the journal. No panels beyond decision 4's two reviews.

## 2. The bet on one page

**Keep verbatim from Snapback 2** (LLP 2000.000 §5.1): the rule language
with its uniformity and hoist analysis, `decided_true` and the
structural-fact refusals; one data language, Quarry, with `--` comments,
`require … else CODE`, `shape`, `merge`, `walk`, `chunk`; the value model
and its conformance corpus; typed read and write states with no silent state
and the refusal envelope `{code, family, site, message, rewrite, guide}`; the
successor classifier (widening additive, checked narrowing, exact projection
ownership); `deliver`'s engine-computed audience, tombstones and
`bootstrap`; `schedule`, `job`, `chunk` and rehearsal; personas; `init`,
`dev`, `check`, `doctor`, `run`, `test`, `why`, `trace`, `guide`; H0
`deploy`, `rollback`, `secret`; channels; assets; the word-prefix index.

**Flip 1 — certify authorization, meter resources** (LLP 2000.000 §6). The
certificate keeps every read site's shape, uniformity, completeness class
and prediction eligibility, and stops pricing bytes and steps. A
deterministic meter charges examined rows, index entries, rule probes,
steps, heap, output and rows written as the program runs, on the server and
# LLP 2000.000: Snapback 3 — keep the core, flip two substrates, start at hub scale

**Type:** RFC
**Status:** Draft
**Role:** Second opinion on LLP 2000; since 2026-09-12 also the design brief Snapback 4 builds (LLP 3000)
**Systems:** Snapback 3 product architecture, bounded computation, client data model, client API vocabulary, language and tooling, effects runtime, verification (simulation), performance budgets, process
**Author:** Claude Fable 5.1, at Charlie Cheever's request
**Date:** 2026-09-12
**Revised:** 2026-09-12 (adopted as the Snapback 4 design brief by LLP 3000 decision 3); 2026-09-12 (initial)
**Related:** [LLP 2000](./2000-snapback3.rfc.md) (parent), [LLP 3000](./3000-snapback4.explainer.md) (the Snapback 4 root that builds this brief), LLP 0000, [LLP 0329](./0329-agent-native-rebuild-diagnosis.research.md), [LLP 0329.014.004](./0329.014.004-bounded-data-programs-v1-design.rfc.md), [LLP 0329.016](./0329.016-agentic-dev-loop-from-scratch.rfc.md), [LLP 1000](./1000-snapback2.explainer.md), [LLP 1001](./1001-query-engine.spec.md), [LLP 1002](./1002-dev-loop.spec.md), [LLP 1012](./1012-local-first-v1.spec.md), [LLP 1018.003](./1018.003-truthful-and-composed-client.decision.md), [LLP 1018.008](./1018.008-elegance-after-the-corrections.research.md), [LLP 1018.008.007](./1018.008.007-affordable-offline-acquisition.research.md), [LLP 1018.008.008](./1018.008.008-locality-of-application-changes.research.md), [LLP 1018.008.009](./1018.008.009-residual-ergonomic-gaps.research.md), [LLP 1019](./1019-derived-delivery-custody.rfc.md), [LLP 1020](./1020-day-two-evolution-and-legible-ceilings.rfc.md), [LLP 1023](./1023-clique-builder-jump.rfc.md), [LLP 1024](./1024-single-lane-delivered-search.rfc.md), [LLP 1025](./1025-digest-final-emission-at-hub-scale.research.md), `rules/RULES.md`, `rules/NOT-DOING.md`, `benchmarks/runs/2026-09-05-clique-full-3x3/holistic-scores.md`

## 1. Summary

LLP 2000 is right that the whole application is the unit of design, right
that ownership comes before abstraction, and right to ask for deterministic
simulation and an early real deployment. Its weakness is that it treats the
two structural choices that produced Snapback 2's costs as open research
questions, and then proposes a first experiment at exactly the scale where
those costs are invisible. This document takes a position on both, names
what to keep from Snapback 2 verbatim, and rewrites the first slice so it
can fail for the right reasons.

The position, in one paragraph. Snapback 2's *core* is excellent and should
be copied: the rule language and its uniformity analysis, the structural-fact
family, one data language, typed states with no silence, refusals that paste
a rewrite, personas, the successor classifier, the derived per-recipient
log, the loop verbs and their sub-second budgets. Two *substrates* under
that core are where the record shows the hours went, and both should be
flipped rather than re-investigated: (1) the static certificate should keep
certifying **authorization and shape** and stop pricing **bytes and steps**,
which move to a deterministic runtime meter with honest `capped`/abort
outcomes; and (2) the client should hold **a replica of the viewer's declared
partition** — the local-first shape every shipped sync engine and Snapback
2's own `deliver` log converge on — instead of a per-query result cache
proven by receipts. Those two flips dissolve most of the machinery that
leaked into the application (receipts, coverage, halos, validators, grants,
ID slices, drains, `withHistory`, follower inboxes, per-table retention
words) and turn the 28-second acquisition into a bulk stream whose floor is
the device's write speed. The first slice should be built on the **hub
fixture** (1,000 memberships, 500 follows, 100,000 messages), with the
day-two change designed before the day-one screen, a native client in the
first slice, and a deterministic simulator as the primary test harness.

Three claims this document makes that LLP 2000 does not:

1. **The costs have two named causes, and both are decisions, not
   mysteries.** Byte-priced static certification and the receipt-proven
   result cache account for every ergonomic residual in LLP 1018.008.009
   except documentation. §2 shows the mapping.
2. **The first experiment must include the hub.** Snapback 2 was "elegant on
   day one and hostile on day two" (LLP 1020) and broke at 1,000 memberships
   (LLPs 1023, 1024, 1025). A two-principal slice will pass and teach nothing.
3. **The process is part of the product.** Seventy-one LLPs, over two
   thousand commits, and seven ceiling raises in fourteen days produced a
   sound system nobody can hold in their head. Snapback 3 needs one owner on
   the core, a simulator instead of a test matrix, and budgets anchored to
   competitors so that raising one means admitting a loss.

## 2. What the record says, read as causes

The evidence LLP 2000 §2 cites is right; its reading is too gentle. Every
row below is a Snapback 2 cost recorded in the corpus, with the substrate
that produced it. Numbers are the corpus's own and retain its scope limits.

| Recorded cost | Where | Cause |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Adding `editedAt: time ?` (44 bytes × 244 retained copies = 10,736) pushed `messages.searchAll` to 4,199,688 closure bytes against 4,194,304; the builder shrank a page from 20 to 19 | LLP 1018.008.008; 0.0.21 day-two diary | **Static byte pricing.** Closure bytes are a bound on what a receipt could ship; the product paid for the bound, not the work |
| The 1,000-membership digest fold certifies at 9,922,698 heap and 8,691,704 closure bytes and is refused; the recipient-keyed form is admitted at 2,733,253 / 1,848,418 | LLP 1025 §3 | **Static byte pricing** (heap = steps × 32 + closure scratch). A runtime meter would have run the fold and reported actual bytes |
| A message page is 50, not 200, because a row "prices at 16,016 bytes" and 200 exceed the 1 MiB output ceiling | LLP 1012 §3 | **Static byte pricing** of the worst-case row width |
| A per-recipient search projection is refused at 20,863,641 reserved steps against 1,000,000 | LLP 1024 §1 | **Static step pricing** of a derivation the engine would perform incrementally |
prove the distinction. Completed point/role witnesses filter each occurrence,
including assertions in requirements, before grouping; helper/shape/spread and
reference-action effects invalidate evidence. Inline and mandatory helper point
updates count as lookup attempts, never existence proof. Cascade edits lead with
the product choice; terminal arithmetic is verbose, with JSON evidence retained.
Literal sync repairs, reserved names and grouped cursor notes complete LANG3.

LLP 3000.036.002 §12 records the amendment. Fresh Codex and Muse round 8 both
READY on matching final digests; every received review is retained verbatim.
All five crates are covered: combined run completed CLI/core before its one-hour
timeout; full language and device/simulation reruns pass. Language 2,091,
device 1,562 and simulation 749 tests pass; core docs 17 and affected clients 42
pass. Native20k and device0–999 have no disagreement; Lean20k reproduces only
four existing signatures. The 100-schema survey stays 100/100, notes371→357,
with every delta explained. Packed local-client/compiler checks cover updated
help, cascade rendering and the actual P18 cases. Clean web measurement is
2,372,437 B raw /723,160 B gzip /545,311 B Brotli, budget723,660 B = gzip+500.
ref-check and docs-budget pass. Root inventories/guide report three missing
legacy Snapback1 generated-file prerequisites, not LANG3 content failures.
Receipts and scope limits: reports/wave14-lang3/ and WORKSTREAM-REPORT.md.
No push, version bump, Mac claim or publication qualification is made here.

500 B. Whole-artifact growth from 0.4.30 is 115 B gzip, without a runtime ceiling

note in app 079. The authoring census regenerates to 497 mutations with the same
94 eligible private write sites. Live-parent authority remains in its separate
workstream; it is excluded from this release. No push or publication is authorized
by this integration task. Qualification details are retained in the integration
report; a running/failed check is never represented as a passing qualification.
